6 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is our view of the macro and market landscape heading
into 2027 and beyond?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
A supply side-led economic expansion continues, driven by capital investment in the AI
buildout, solid consumer spending and fiscal tailwinds still blowing, albeit with less force.
This creates a backdrop that keeps nominal growth well-above trend and, importantly,

above the interest cost on national debt.
The interest rate backdrop largely remains the same as of today, but, instead of the
sharp increases that we experienced in 2026, the curve should settle down, and yields
should remain relatively range bound. The Fed is likely to tap on the breaks a couple of
more times before reassessing new inflation, growth and employment trends later in
2027.
Concerns surrounding credit risk and "circular" financing are likely to rise the further we

move into the AI buildout, but we do not expect a default cycle to develop. Rather, we
expect the sharp backup in yields to place pressure namely on lower-quality credits in
areas having a difficult time building the cash flow necessary to pay the higher interest
costs.
We expect a broadening out within the market but technology to still maintain its
heavyweight positioning. As we move from training and evolving the large language
models to agentic, and physical AI new leaders are likely to develop. In addition, we

anticipate AI adopters such as biotechnology, transport, logistics, advance manufacturing
and financial companies to enter new growth and productivity phases.
Lastly, overall earnings growth in the U.S. should be low to mid double-digit percent
stages in 2027, which is about half of the growth from 2026 thus far but still around
double from normal levels.
Credit risks developing and higher energy prices for longer are the main risks to our bull
case for Equities extending further into 2027 and beyond.

Index Definitions
ecurities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

University of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index is a monthly survey of consumer confidence levels in the United States conducted by the University of Michigan.
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence measures what consumers are feeling about their expected financial situation, whether that's optimistic or pessimistic.
Consumer Price Index is a statistical measure that tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative "market basket" of everyday goods and

services.
Nasdaq Composite Index is a stock market measure that tracks the performance of more than 3,000 stocks listed on the Nasdaq exchange.
MSCI Emerging Markets c is a stock market index that tracks the performance of large-cap and mid-cap companies across 24 developing and fast-growing nations.
MSCI All-Country World Index ex USA is a market-capitalization-weighted stock market index that tracks large- and mid-cap equity performance across developed and emerging markets

worldwide, excluding the United States.
MSCI Europe, Australasia, and the Far East (EAFE) Index is a stock market index that measures the performance of large- and mid-cap companies across 21 developed.
Dow Jones Industrial Average Index is a stock market index that tracks 30 major public companies listed on the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) and Nasdaq.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index is an alternative version of the standard S&P 500 that gives every constituent company an identical weight of 0.2% during each quarterly rebalance, rather

than weighting companies by their market capitalization.
Russell 2000 Index is a stock market index that tracks the performance of approximately 2,000 small-cap (small-capitalization) public companies in the United States.
S&P 500 sub-sectors and industry groups Global Industry Classification Standard (GICS®)/S&P 500 Total Return Index, including Information Technology Total Return (TR) USD;
Consumer Discretionary TR USD; Industrials TR USD; Real Estate TR USD; Communication Services TR USD; Materials  TR USD; Financials TR USD; Consumer Staples TR USD; Utilities TR USD;

Investing involves risk, including the possible loss of principal. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.

This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase

or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and
investment advisory services, including the type of advice and assistance provided, the fees charged, and the rights and obligations of the parties. It is important to understand the differences,
particularly when determining which service or services to select. For more information about these services and their differences, speak with your Merrill financial advisor.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

2 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Setting the Stage for 2027
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
Typically, a new administration sets the stage in their first year with stimulative policy
changes that play out in the second year after the new policies have been enacted into
law and begin to have their impact. This pattern shows up in historical data with the
average rate of gross domestic product (GDP) growth highest in the second year of

presidential terms compared to the other three years of the presidential term. At the
same time, equity market returns tend to be lowest on average in the second year of
presidential terms, which seems to be at odds with the fact that growth is strongest.
This apparent inconsistency can be attributed to the fact that the stock market is a
leading indicator that anticipates stronger growth and higher profits well before they
actually arrive. Once they do, as they have in 2026, the markets are looking ahead to the

inevitable slowing from the policy-induced peak in growth that typically arrives after the
midterm election year. The midterms also create uncertainty about the subsequent policy
environment which damps stocks ahead of the election only to be reversed on average
once the elections are over and the outlook is clarified. This phenomenon is seen in the
fact that on average the stock market has corrected about 20% ahead of midterms only
to rise about 30% from that bottom in the year after.

Q3 GDP data illustrate this pattern. Economic growth picked up through the first three
quarters of 2026 to a peak of 3.4%. Consumer spending growth averaged 3.8% over the
past six months, while the AI investment boom accelerated at a midteens growth rate.
Private sector purchases were growing at just shy of a 5% growth rate. GDP growth is
lower than that because imports of Asia produced technology-gear for the AI buildout
are subtracting a good bit from that underlying 5% domestic demand growth pace.

The new policies aimed at helping consumers through lower taxes and investment
incentives to spur private sector production have boosted growth as expected. Still, this
acceleration is passing, and the fiscal boost is likely to fade in 2027. Profits naturally
have followed this stronger GDP growth higher.
The upward trend in analysts' earnings revisions has started to roll over in our view, and,
while earnings are still growing, the rate of growth is likely to slow from here. In simple

terms, the second derivative of growth has turned negative. From way above trend
toward trend growth is still sufficient for the bull market to continue.
Another aspect of this transition to midcycle aside from the fading midterm
election boost from fiscal measures is the shift of monetary policy from maximum
accommodation to a more neutral policy. The Fed's rate cuts helped the midterm
acceleration in growth. Its shift to removing accommodation with rate hikes this year

adds to the odds of some momentum loss in 2027. That's already apparent as Federal
Reserve (Fed) Chair Warsh noted in his press conference after the September Federal
Open Market Committee meeting. Weaker balance sheet borrowers have seen their
spreads blow out as credit becomes more expensive. So far, the damage is confined to
the lowest-quality borrowers. Before, these spreads were unusually low reflecting easy
money conditions that are now normalizing. It's still early days in the new credit cycle.

Of course, how the market responds to this new Fed tightening cycle depends on how
aggressively the Fed raises rates. Analysts at Ned Davis Research note the S&P 500
has gained an average of 4.4% in the first year of all postwar tightening cycles. This
outcome varies considerably depending on the extent of the tightening. Aggressive
tightening cycles have seen an average decline of 3.6% a year later, whereas gradual
tightenings have seen a 10.5% average gain. In short, as one might expect, too much

tightening can cause a decline in stocks. Currently, the market seems to anticipate a
Investment Implications
Equities should continue to outperform
bonds in 2027 in our view, but
performance is likely to shift in favor of
higher-quality, less leveraged segments
of the market as financial conditions
tighten and earnings momentum slows,
while the accelerating momentum of
the early cycle transitions to midcycle
cruise control.

### Page 3

3 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
December and March nudge up in rates to reverse the easings by the Powell Fed. This
would be consistent with an ongoing equity bull market in 2027. Obviously, inflation
will have a lot to say about whether a mild or aggressive tightening cycle is called for.
With wage pressures benign, productivity strong and the early-cycle acceleration set to
peak, we believe the odds of an aggressive tightening cycle that derails the bull market

are low.
Other policy effects have shaped the current economic cycle. The shift toward more
nationalistic trade policies with tariffs, for example, has drastically impacted the pattern
of trade flows. U.S. imports from China have dropped from about 20% 10 years ago to
less than 10%, according to research from Strategas. Nevertheless, contrary to concerns
of a trade-induced recession, overall trade has continued to grow in line with the global
economy. Rather than deglobalization, the world is undergoing a reglobalization into new

trade spheres that better align with national interests.
According to McKinsey Quarterly, "The 2025 U.S. National Security Strategy, for
example, reinforces economic and supply chain security, industrial capacity, technological
leadership, and capital market strength as national priorities. China's latest five-year plan,
meanwhile, includes technological self-reliance, economic stability and the modernization
of core industries among its key pillars. In this environment, value creation is increasingly

shaped by new trade corridors, growing defense spending, expansion of export controls
and industrial policies, and shifting flows of foreign investment." 1 These new trade
policies are an impetus for growth that is structural, helping to make for a strong
investment cycle.
Obviously, AI is a big spur for investment that has been facilitated by the new policies
such as accelerated depreciation of capital expenditures (capex). McKinsey Research
designates 18 arenas with cutting-edge industries they believe are driving the global

growth story for the next couple of decades. They note companies headquartered in
the U.S. and China account for about 90% of these arenas' market value today. U.S.
companies account for 75% of the market capitalization within the 18 arenas and
generate about 50% of revenues. Given its leading role in the arenas driving growth
into the future, we believe the U.S. is well poised to outperform as the world moves
into this new technological future. The 18 arenas are part of the economy growing by

several multiples of the old economy segments and help explain why U.S. Equities have a
persistent valuation premium over the rest of the world.
Because of the unusually rapid growth of these new technologies associated with the
singularity, while earnings growth may decelerate from its peak, there are structural
reasons to believe earnings are on a new, higher-growth trajectory and unlikely to revert
fully back to historical norms. The new technologies have much higher margins than the

old economy sectors. The combination of faster growth and higher margins bodes well
for the equity market as these new arenas of future economic growth become a bigger
part of the U.S. and global economies.
The midterms have changed views about the relative performance of some segments
of the economy. For example, strong sentiment to slow the buildout of AI data centers
was dramatically evident in the election results. Beneficiaries of the buildout are being

revalued to reflect this slower growth. Disapproval of the Iran War was also registered
in the results. Energy prices have dropped on hopes for a quicker end to the war. This in
turn has lowered inflation expectations and interest rates. Overall, while the midterms
have remixed relative performance between sectors they have not changed the outlook
for continued solid growth and an ongoing bull market in equities.
1 McKinsey Quarterly, Q3 2026, page 52.

### Page 4

4 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
China, the WTO and the Great Convergence No One Expected
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Twenty-five years ago this December, the West made one of the biggest economic bets
of the modern era: It brought China into the WTO, convinced that membership would
transform the communist giant into a more open and market-oriented economy.
The logic seemed impeccable at the time. The Soviet Union had long been vanquished.

American capitalism appeared triumphant. Free trade, deregulation, privatization and
unfettered cross-border flows of goods, services, capital and labor were widely viewed as
essential ingredients of economic prosperity. Globalization ruled.
Fold China into the rules-based system, the thinking went, and economic liberalization
would gradually pull Beijing toward Western norms. China would become a willing
participant in the U.S.-led global economic order. The world would change China, in

other words.
Not quite. At the dawn of the 25th anniversary of China’s WTO accession, the great irony is
not how China became more like the West. It is how the West became more like China.
In the U.S., and across Europe and Asia, governments are increasingly embracing the
elements of China’s economic playbook, which range from state subsidies to the promotion
of national champions to strategic industrial policies, among other initiatives antithetical to

the global economic norms that prevailed when China joined the WTO.
The deployment of trade tariffs, the subsidization of semiconductor production, the
promotion of domestic manufacturing, and the screening of foreign investment are no
longer hallmarks of China alone. They are increasingly features of economic policymaking
in the U.S., the one-time champion of globalization. The European Union, Japan, South
Korea and other nations that once carried the flag for free trade and open markets have

moved much in the same direction. Even the World Bank—a chief architect and guardian of
Western-led globalization—has come around to blessing the merits of industrial policy, a
strategy central to China’s economic ascent.
To be sure, Washington has not embraced China’s economic model wholesale. Neither have
Brussels, Tokyo or Seoul. The political, economic and institutional differences between
these systems and China’s remain vast. But the direction—set by China—is unmistakable.

It was not supposed to be this way. To gain admission to the WTO, China made significant
concessions over 15 years of negotiations. Beijing slashed tariffs on industrial goods and
agricultural products. Various sectors—manufacturing, distribution, telecommunications,
banking and autos—were gradually opened to foreign firms. Commercial laws and regulations
were amended or written, including rules governing intellectual property rights. Nontariff
barriers like quotas, import licenses, dual pricing and opaque industry standards were either

eliminated or phased out. State-owned enterprises were reformed and restructured.
These concessions—and more—were taken as signs that China was serious about
becoming a global stakeholder. It stoked optimism among senior U.S. officials that China
was inevitably bound to converge and embrace Western economic norms. And, for a while,
the bet seemed to pay off.
As China lowered trade and investment barriers at the beginning of the century, U.S. goods

exports soared. American firms poured capital into the mainland, attracted by an enormous
labor pool, lower production costs and what would soon become one of the largest
consumer markets in the world.
China’s massive labor pool-cum-ballooning middle class paid off handsomely for Corporate
America. U.S. affiliate income earned in China rose from just $1.6 billion (B) in 2001 to
nearly $9B by 2010, making China one of the most profitable markets in the world for U.S.

goods and services. American consumers, meanwhile, enjoyed the benefits of low-cost
China imports. And debt-laden America benefited as China recycled its excess savings into
U.S. Treasurys, helping to grease the financial wheels of America.
Investment Implications
We expect China will continue to rely
on exports and investment to drive
growth amid weak consumption and
domestic demand. Manufacturing-
heavy economies like Germany
remain particularly exposed,

reinforcing our neutral stance toward
Europe in portfolios.

### Page 5

5 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
But just as WTO accession helped boost China’s imports, it also supercharged China’s
export machine. Rather than shrink, as some optimists had predicted, America’s
merchandise trade deficit with China more than doubled between 2001 ($83.2B) and 2005
($202.8B) and continued rising thereafter. As the decade wore on, U.S. manufacturing jobs
and wages came under intense pressure owing to expanding China imports and the shift

in U.S. production to lower-cost China. Entire supply chains migrated across the Pacific,
decimating many manufacturing communities across the U.S.
China began producing and exporting at a scale the world had never experienced. In the
span of just a decade, China’s share of world manufacturing output soared from 8.7%
in 2000 to 18.1% in 2010, when the nation eclipsed the U.S. as the world’s largest
manufacturer of goods. Four years later, China was outproducing all of Europe—and has

never looked back (Exhibit 1A).
Today, China sits at the center of global manufacturing and trade, competing not simply in
textiles, toys and other low-value products but also in more technology-driven industries
like electric vehicles, drones, machinery and clean energy. China now accounts for roughly
28% of global manufacturing, according to the World Bank, well ahead of second place
America (17%). Meanwhile, the nation’s share of world exports—just 4% in 2001—now

stands at 15%, roughly on par with the combined market share of second-place America
and Germany (Exhibit 1B). The world is so awash with China goods that the nation is on
course to post another annual merchandise trade surplus in excess of $1 trillion.
Not surprisingly, China’s grip on world trade has significantly dimmed the brimming
optimism that greeted the nation’s WTO entry a quarter-century ago. Today, the consensus
is that China’s trade surplus has been aided and abetted by nonmarket practices and

policies that include forced technology transfers, currency manipulations, state subsidies
to industry, restrictions on foreign investment in strategic sectors, intellectual property
infringements and resource protectionism. Rather than dismantle its state-led, nonmarket
and mercantilist economy, China has tacked in the other direction, notably under the
leadership of Xi Jinping.
For investors, the implications have been profound. We now live in a world where

governments increasingly decide which industries are strategically indispensable, which
technologies must remain domestically controlled and which companies require public
financing. The “Commanding Heights”—or who controls the economy—have shifted
from the private sector to the public sector. In this world, investment returns are not only
determined by traditional metrics like earnings, the cost of capital and valuations but also
by geopolitics, energy costs, trade protectionism and national security. The world today is

far different from the world when China joined the WTO.
Suffice it to say that the 25th anniversary of China’s entry into the WTO won’t be met
with much celebration in the West. The consensus in 2001 was that the world would
change China. Twenty-five years later, it’s China that has changed the world. It’s the great
convergence no one saw coming.

### Page 6

6 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What are we reviewing to gauge the capital investment needed for the proposed Artificial
Intelligence buildout and the return required to cover its costs?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Officer
Capex by the 15 largest data center developers are expected to top $1.4 trillion in
2027, which is somewhere between 3.5% and 4% of U.S. GDP . A large portion of the
capital investment is earmarked for compute. The total capacity of gigawatts (GW)

under construction is around 34GW of Informaton Technology capacity, with the U.S.
accounting for approximately 70% of this, according to BloombergNEF. Given the
massive compute investment, we continue to analyze closely the total debt financing
announced, the approximate cost of capital of the capital providers, the associated
revenues needed to match or exceed cost estimates, and the individual credit spreads
(as well as credit default swaps spreads) of the larger hyperscalers and chip makers to

better assess the overall return on investment of the AI buildout.
BloombergNEF estimates, that at the beginning of this year, the highest-rated data
center bonds offered yields below 5%, which is now closer to around 7% today. In
addition, AI-tied firms have raised over $460B in debt financing this year, which is
more than double that from 2025. Finally, according to BloombergNEF and Bloomberg
Intelligence, as of September 18, 2026, it is estimated that the 15 largest publicly listed

data center companies would need to produce $2.5 trillion in revenues in 2032 in order
to achieve a return on invested capital equal to the company-level weighted average cost
of capital. This is about 5 times more than the expected revenue in 2026.
Recently, the concern across the capital markets is that there is not enough financing
capital to go around in order to fund the expected buildout, which is one of the primary
reasons why credit spreads have widened and yields have risen. We view this as a natural

development of such large-scale infrastructure transformations. This is the reason why
we continue to watch the overall health of balance sheets of the builders, the level of
profitability and margins, and the order backlogs/cancellations in the next few years. We
still believe we are closer to the middle of the AI buildout than to the end. Therefore, we
view larger-scale weakness in the higher-quality segments of the AI chain as potential
buying opportunities for investors who are underexposed to these areas.

Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.
Nonfinancial assets, such as closely held businesses, real estate, fine art, oil, gas and mineral properties, and timber, farm and ranch land, are complex in nature and involve risks including total

## Document 317: Cliff Notes_ the source and persistence of inflation (2026-11-13)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ the source and persistence of inflation (2026-11-13).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: the source and persistence of inflation
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
11:16 October 30 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia this week, all eyes were on the September CPI. Headline inflation rose 0.8% (4.3%yr) in
the month, largely owing to a 9.4% jump in fuel prices and a seasonal surge in domestic and
international holiday travel & accommodation. The underlying inflation pulse also proved hotter than

anticipated for Q3 overall, up 1.0% (3.7%yr).

### Page 2

While some parts of the basket are seeing disinflation – housing in particular looks to be cooling faster
than expected as builders lure sales with discounts – other components are still showing persistence.
Highlighting this is market services ex volatiles, which the RBA watches closely as an indicator of price
pressures related to domestic capacity. In Q3, this metric rose 1.2% (3.2%yr), and the monthly detail
points to momentum holding up – in line with our expectations for another chunky read in Q4.

With inflation running well above the RBA’s August forecasts, and some of the energy risks flagged
then materialising, we continue to expect the Monetary Policy Board will deliver a 25bp rate hike to
4.85% in November. Beyond November, another rate hike in February is a risk, but is not currently part
of our base case.
Across the Tasman, the RBNZ this week surprised market expectations for a 25bp rate hike, deciding
instead to wait for further information before taking the next step with policy. The decision was made

4:3, with the Governor in the ‘on hold’ camp. A 25bp hike in December to 3.00% and further tightening
in 2027 (Westpac NZ forecasts a peak of 4.00% in September) seems probable; however, each
decision will depend on fuel prices, inflation expectations and evidence of second-round pricing
pressures, along with the evolution of global economic risks.
In the US, the FOMC also held rates steady at its October meeting. Based on prior comments by key
members, the decision was not a surprise, and neither were Governor Cook and Cleveland Fed

President Hammack’s dissents in favour of a 25bp hike. The Committee remains positive on economic
activity, characterising it in the statement as “expanding at a strong pace” (an upgrade from “solid” in
September). Chair Warsh also subsequently emphasised the breadth of economic growth outside of
tech-related investment (evinced by Q3 GDP the day after; see below).
The Committee refrained from spotlighting key price risks in the statement. However, inflation “remains

elevated” and the FOMC are determined to achieve a “timely” and sustainable return of annual inflation
to 2.0%yr after 6 years above target. This guidance and the “accumulation of more information will help
the Committee determine the policy stance” suggests another 25bp hike is likely in December.
Thereafter, the FOMC will remain attentive to any broadening in inflation and related risks through
2027; although, in the absence of another shock, we expect them to remain on hold.

The Q3 GDP report certainly supported Chair Warsh’s view on the breadth of growth, the 3.4%
annualised gain for GDP benefitting from a 3.8% annualised increase in household consumption,
12.7% growth in non-residential investment as well as a robust increase in government spending.
Notably though, the contribution from business investment was effectively offset by net exports’ drag
given most of the componentry related to AI infrastructure investment is imported. This is not to say that

US firms won’t benefit from the investment once complete, but simply that much of the initial return
goes to the foreign businesses who provide the necessary capital equipment.
On consumption, the monthly PCE data implies downside risks remain for the consumer, the increase
in nominal spending seen in the month requiring dissaving and, on a real basis, consumption flat in the
month following a 0.5% gain in August. Cost-of-living pressures and uncertainty over the outlook is

### Page 3

Related articles
expected to see household consumption growth average around trend over the coming year, albeit with
considerable volatility quarter to quarter. This should see PCE inflation slow back towards its 2.0%yr
medium-term target as the FOMC desires, from 3.6%yr and 3.1%yr for the headline and core
measures respectively as of September.
Across the Atlantic, the ECB also left its key policy rates unchanged in October. Of note from the
meeting communications, energy prices continue to pose upside risks for inflation, but also downside

risks for growth. "While the full effects of the energy shock are still unfolding, underlying inflation has
remained broadly stable so far and wages do not show a material response". "Financing conditions
have [also] tightened", "which is likely to weigh on inflation and growth". In the press conference,
President Lagarde noted that, between the October and December meetings, a significant amount of
new economic information will be released, allowing forecasts and risks assessments to be tested.

Based on the October meeting communications, we continue to expect a rate hike at the December
meeting but believe this will be prove to be the last hike of the cycle. Note though, the spectrum of risks
Europe faces is broad and skewed towards inflation in the near term, hence the Council’s guidance will
remain hawkish. President Lagarde also announced today that she will step down as ECB President in
February ahead of the scheduled conclusion of her term next October.

## Document 335: EcoNews of 2 November 2026 (2026-11-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/EcoNews of 2 November 2026 (2026-11-02).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 2

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
5
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.40 / 2 November 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
In brief. Data releases confirmed resilient to strong growth and
above-forecast inflation in most advanced economies, with France lag-
ging behind and earning a rating outlook downgrade from Fitch as a
result. Fed, ECB and BoJ remained on hold, as expected, and on track
for December hikes. Oil continues to fluctuate between escalation fears

and hopes for a return to the negotiating table. The tech sell-off in
equity markets intensified despite strong earnings, as markets balk at
large capex plans. This helped bond yields ease from recent highs.
EUROZONE
Resilience confirmed despite tightening credit. GDP expanded by a
stronger-than-expected 0.4% q/q in Q3 (0.2% ex-Ireland), with Spain,
Germany and the Netherlands contributing to the bulk of non-Irish
growth. We increase our growth forecast to 1.6% in 2027 (+0.1pp),

driven by further improving sentiment in manufacturing. Bank lending
conditions tightened further in Q3 for businesses, especially SMEs,
driven by France and Germany, while corporate and housing-loan de -
mand weakened. The ECB kept its deposit rate at 2.5% and signaled it
was firmly on track for a (likely final) December hike. President Lagarde
also confirmed she would step down early, on February 7, setting off
the succession race. EU leaders are expected to decide jointly on the

three Board members departing between January and May (President,
chief economist, and market operations), likely at their mid-December
meeting. Official candidates to succeed Lagarde are BIS Head Pablo
Hernandez de Cos and former Dutch central bank Governor Klaas Knot.
To watch next week: October flash CPI (Wednesday).
France: Another growth setback, with silver linings
GDP contracted by 0.1% q/q in Q3 as a sharp drop in aeronauti -
cal production hit exports and investment. However, services out -

put grew by a robust 0.3% q/q, and household consumption also
grew more strongly than forecasted (+0.4%). While 2026 will ine -
vitably be knocked down (to 0.3%), we forecast 0.8% for 2027.
Nevertheless, Fitch issued an unscheduled rating outlook downgrade
to negative territory, on grounds that fiscal, growth and ad -
justment prospects have deteriorated since its August review.
A new review could be concluded as early as February. Harmonised
inflation accelerated to 3.8% y/y in October, with both energy and

non-energy contributing. Meanwhile, producer price inflation accele -
rated to 8.2% y/y in September. Insee’s index of household confidence
fell to 80, matching its 2013 all-time low, driven by a decline in ex -
pectations of its own financial situation. Private payroll employment
declined by 26,000 over Q3 (-0.1%). However, in previous quarters, the
decline in payroll employment was more than offset by the increase in
self-employment (not released until November 27).

To watch next week: September industrial production (Thursday); Sep-
tember foreign trade (Friday).
Germany: Growth holds up as inflation slows
GDP grew by 0.2% q/q in Q3, with consumption rising slightly but in -
vestment declining and export growth slowing. HICP inflation fell from
3.3% to 2.8% in October following energy-tax cuts and fuel discounts,
but core inflation also slowed, to 2.1%. Business confidence (Ifo survey)
reached its highest level in more than three years, led by manufactu -

ring, which is back in positive territory for the first time since 2023.
By contrast, consumer confidence deteriorated to its lowest since May,
due to growing concerns about inflation, which are weighing on consu-
mers’ spending intentions.
To watch next week: September retail sales (Monday); September fac-
tory orders (Thursday); September industrial production (Friday).
Italy: Stagnant growth meets energy-driven inflation surge
GDP was flat in Q3, missing expectations, as positive domestic demand

was offset by net exports. Economic sentiment improved, but HICP in -
flation jumped from 4.2% to 5.6% in October, its highest level since
September 2023, mainly because of regulated energy prices; core in -
flation also rose, to a more moderate 2%. More positively, economic
sentiment rose to 100.2 in October 2026 (from 99.4 in September),
despite the Eurozone decline. The improvement was driven by services
and construction. The Italian cabinet approved a budget focused on

middle-class tax cuts, financed partly by levies on banks, energy and
gambling companies. x
UNITED KINGDOM
A cautious budget buys breathing space. The budget offered 98 small
cost-of-living alleviation measures and £4.1 billion in household ener-
gy cost relief, while only modestly increasing taxes, including a tem -
porary rise in the bank surcharge. Its short-term stance is slightly ex -
pansionary, while major structural decisions on defence, pensions and

welfare have been postponed. The official budget watchdog, OBR, cut
its 2027 growth forecast to 1%, and firms’ inflation expectations conti-
nued to rise. UK gilts outperformed and equities advanced after the
budget. GBP rose to a six-week high against the dollar and a 19-month
high versus the euro as markets price a near-certain Bank of England
rate hike (to 4%) at this week meeting. We agree but, unlike market
pricing, we do not expect it to hike further.

To watch next week: Bank of England interest-rate decision (Thursday).
UNITED STATES
Fed awaiting more information despite strong growth. The Federal Re-
serve held its target range at 3.75–4.0%, with two members voting for a
25 bp increase, as Chair Warsh highlighted stronger growth, favourable
employment conditions and inflation that remains too high. Q3 GDP
accelerated to 3.4% annualised, powered by resilient consumption and
a 12.7% jump in business investment. Despite jobless claims remaining

near multi-decade lows, compensation costs adjusted for inflation de-
clined 0.4%, marking the second consecutive quarter in which labour
cost growth trailed price increases.
To watch next week: Midterm elections (Tuesday); October ISM ma -
nufacturing (Monday) and non-manufacturing (Wednesday); Q3 non-
farm productivity (Thursday); October non-farm payrolls (Friday).
JAPAN
BoJ on hold with underlying inflation “consistent with the 2% tar -
get”. The Bank of Japan kept its policy rate at 1.25%, with one dissent

in favour of a hike. Its outlook judged underlying inflation to be
consistent with the 2% target, a major shift in its assessment, although
Governor Ueda mentioned risks on both sides in his remarks, stres -
sing in particular the risk of inflation overshooting the target. It signals
continued gradual monetary adjustment, with a next hike expected in
December. Alongside the rate hold, the BOJ announced it would near -
ly triple the pace of ETF sales starting in January, to approximately

JPY 860 billion per year. Japanese equities shrugged off the announ -
cement.
To watch next week: Minutes of the September Bank of Japan meeting
(Thursday); September wage growth (Friday).

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.40 / 2 November 2026

EMERGING ECONOMIES
ASIA
China: Monetary policy reform and no growth signals
The PBOC disclosed a 1.25% overnight reverse-repo rate and an -
nounced that the overnight rate would become its principal policy
lever, starting 2nd November. It also pledged to develop cross-border
renminbi payments and introduced a liquidity backstop for non-bank

financial institutions. The 5th Plenum of the 20 th Central Committee of
the CCP focused on party discipline and crackdown on corrupt cadres,
without broader policy signals. Chinese equities had their worst month
since 2016, falling 8.5% in October, as doubts over AI earnings deepe -
ned investor pessimism. The PMIs published on 30 th October suggest
economic activity is losing momentum; in the manufacturing sector,
the RatingDog PMI fell slightly to 52 (from 52.1) while the official PMI

fell more sharply and dropped below 50 (49.3 vs. 50.1 in the previous
month).
South Korea and Taiwan: AI boom lifts growth even higher
In Korea, GDP grew by 1.0% q/q and 3.4% y/y in Q3, supported by boo -
ming semiconductor exports linked to global AI demand. The expansion
was uneven, however, as private-consumption growth slowed marke -
dly. In Taiwan, GDP surged by 3.2% q/q and 14.4% y/y in Q3, beating
expectations and accelerating from the previous quarter as technology

firms expanded capacity to meet strong demand.
EMEA
Poland: Lower inflation gives monetary policy some room
Headline inflation declined from 4.0% to 3.5% in October as electricity,
gas and transport fuel prices rose more slowly, helped by a temporary
fuel-price cap. Rates are likely to remain unchanged in the near term,
although renewed zloty weakness means a hike cannot be ruled out.
Saudi Arabia: Reserves fall as regional pressures intensify
Official reserve assets declined by 4.4% m/m in September to

SAR 1.75 tn, their lowest level since December, with foreign-currency
assets recording particularly sharp monthly and annual declines.
Türkiye: Pressure on lira and FX reserves
The lira headed for its largest monthly fall in 15 months and cen -
tral-bank reserves came under renewed pressure. This is the conse -
quence of the warning by MSCI that Türkiye could lose its emer -
ging-market status if measures to improve transparency and market
supervision following the Fund scandal are deemed insufficient.

LATIN AMERICA
Brazil’s post-election rout: stocks and real plunge, yields surge. Bra -
zilian markets, expecting a Flavio Bolsonaro victory, sold off sharply
after Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva won a fourth presidential term. The Ibo-
vespa fell 14% Monday — its largest daily drop since COVID-19 — and
ended the week down 18%. The real fell over 11% intraday and ended
the week down 8% against the euro. Meanwhile the yield on Brazil’s
10-year government bonds rose 250 bp to 15.5%, reaching its highest

level in over a decade. The selloff reflects fiscal concerns over pledges
to raise the minimum wage and Bolsa familia benefits and to offer free
public transport on weekends and bank holidays. Finance Minister Da-
rio Durigan’s pledge to seek a higher 2027 primary surplus, without a
new target, did little to reassure markets.
COMMODITIES
The oil market continues to fluctuate between escalation fears and
hopes for a return to the negotiating table. Brent prices were down by

5% over the week, reaching USD 109/b on Monday. Tensions on physical
supply continue to decline. Ditto for diesel crack spreads. On the back
of geopolitical tensions, lower-than-requested EU stock level and spe-
culation over a cold snap in Europe in November, European gas prices
(TTF) recorded another strong weekly rise and have breached the
EUR 100/MWh for the first time since Dec 2022). The Asian gas bench-
mark (JKM) is following the same trend but at a slower pace (4.6% w/w

to USD 32 /MBtu, equivalent to around EUR 96.3 /MWh).
TO WATCH NEXT WEEK
Global: Manufacturing PMIs for EM and final DM prints (Monday); final
services and composite PMIs (Wednesday).

### Page 2

Weekly Economics
U.S. Week Ahead
Existing Home Sales • Thursday
Housing market conditions remain highly unfavorable. Mortgage
rates are the primary headwind, and they have climbed even higher
alongside the rise in longer-term yields. At the same time, low
supply is preventing a broad correction in home prices, leaving
prospective buyers squeezed on two fronts.
We expect that existing home sales slid 3.4% in August to an
annualized rate of 3.92 million. Behind this forecast is ongoing

weakness in mortgage applications and a recent bounce in
mortgage rates, which rose from 6.43% at the beginning of July to
6.66% by month-end.
We do not expect affordability conditions to meaningfully improve
in the near term. Supply is likely to remain constrained while high
mortgage rates discourage mobility. And mortgage rates are apt
to remain elevated amid firm economic growth expectations and
heavy government and corporate debt issuance.

### Page 3

Economics Week Ahead Economics
CPI • Friday
Headline inflation looks to have picked up last month. We estimate
the CPI rose 0.40% in August, with gasoline prices increasing a little
over 4% as renewed tensions in the Middle East pushed oil higher.
Grocery prices also likely rebounded after modestly declining in July.
Outside of food and energy, price pressures appear little changed.
We expect the core CPI to rise 0.23%, essentially matching July's

pace. Core goods inflation should remain firm, led by another solid
increase in used vehicle prices as consumer prices continue to
catch up to the trend indicated at wholesale auctions. Meantime,
household furnishings and other tariff-sensitive categories appear
to be stabilizing after declining earlier this year.
Core services inflation should be little changed with another 0.2%
monthly increase. But underneath the surface, we expect a modest
pickup in travel (led by lodging away from home) to offset slightly

cooler medical care and housing cost growth.
All told, we expect the report to show that while headline inflation
remains influenced by the ebbs and flows of the conflict in the
Middle East, core inflation remains contained. Should our forecast
prove correct, core CPI will ease to 2.4% year-over-year, in line with
its 6-month annualized rate but above the more recent 3-month
annualized pace of 1.7%.

### Page 4

Weekly Economics
G10 Week Ahead
European Central Bank Monetary Policy Meeting •
Thursday
We expect the European Central Bank (ECB) to raise the Deposit
Rate by 25 bps to 2.50% next week. August headline inflation
remained elevated at 3.3% year-over-year, though softer core
and services inflation suggest underlying pressures continue to
moderate. Still, the more hawkish tone of the July minutes and
renewed rise in energy prices should keep policymakers cautious.

The recent escalation in the Middle East has pushed fuel prices
at the pump close to their 2022 peaks, while natural gas storage
remains below historical levels, leaving the Eurozone vulnerable
to further energy price increases. The ECB is unlikely to respond
aggressively if the shock remains confined to energy given the
downside risks to growth. However, a broader rise in food inflation
linked to El Niño would be more concerning, as highly visible prices

could lift inflation expectations and generate second-round effects.
For now, resilient Q3 activity, elevated headline inflation and
contained wage growth support one final hike. The ECB will likely
retain and emphasize its scenario-based outlook in the updated
staff projections given continued uncertainty around the conflict
and energy prices. The policy path beyond September will depend
on whether energy and food pressures persist and begin to spread,

which could leave room for further tightening.

Economics Week Ahead Economics
EM Week Ahead
Mexico CPI • Wednesday
We expect Mexico’s August inflation rate to edge higher, with
headline CPI rising to 3.34% year-over-year (0.27% month-over-
month) from 3.26% year-over-year in early August. Part of the
pickup likely reflects less favorable base effects and a smaller
disinflationary contribution from food prices. While agricultural
prices continued to help restrain inflation in mid-August, the drag

was notably less pronounced than in July. Core inflation should
remain elevated at 3.98% year-over-year (0.25% month-over-
month).
While inflation remains within Banxico’s midpoint 3% target,
persistent core and services inflation suggest convergence toward
the midpoint of the target could prove gradual. For now, we
continue to expect Banxico to keep rates on hold, with the policy
rate remaining at 6.50% through the rest of 2026 and into early
2027 as policymakers await clearer evidence that underlying

inflation pressures are easing sustainably. While rate cuts remain
a possibility in 2027, the current inflation backdrop argues for
patience in the near term.

Brazil CPI • Friday
We expect Brazil’s August inflation rate to slow to 4.20% year-over-
year, helped by lower electricity, food and fuel prices. The main
driver should be a temporary reduction in residential electricity bills
stemming from a one-off credit, while recent data already point to
a notable easing in price pressures. Mid-August CPI came in weaker
than expected, with inflation falling to 4.24% year-over-year amid
declines in electricity, food, fuel and airfare prices. Beyond these

temporary factors, restrictive monetary policy continues to weigh
on economic activity, as evidenced by the recent slowdown in Q2
GDP growth. However, underlying inflation risks remain. A still-
tight labor market and government measures aimed at supporting
consumption could keep services inflation sticky, making progress
toward the 3.0% inflation target more gradual.
Overall, the inflation outlook remains consistent with additional
policy easing. We expect COPOM to deliver a 25 bps rate cut in

September, bringing the Selic Rate to 13.75%. Beyond September,
policymakers are likely to proceed cautiously as they assess
incoming inflation data and the impact of the election cycle on
inflation expectations. Our base case remains for a pause after
September, with additional easing more likely in 2027.

## Document 390: NZ Q3 Labour market data - Potential RBNZ implications (2026-11-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/NZ Q3 Labour market data - Potential RBNZ implications (2026-11-12).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#labour #newzealand
NZ Q3 Labour market data - Potential RBNZ implications
By Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist, Westpac New Zealand
13:16 November 04 2026 Share
The Q3 labour market statistics were weaker than expected. We discuss potential
implications for the RBNZ noting that a December hike remains more likely than not at this
point.
Q3 Labour market data: potential RBNZ implications
The Q3 labour market data were weaker than RBNZ and our expectations.

### Page 2

Today's labour market data seem unequivocally weaker than expected by either us or the RBNZ. While
likely not a gamechanger for the cycle overall, it will add ammunition to the arguments of the MPC
doves that a pause in interest rate rises will make sense soon - perhaps in December or in the New
Year. It’s likely the case that a bit much was priced into December, so the change in market
probabilities in the wake of today’s data makes sense - although my base case remains that a
December rate rise remains more likely than not.
The message from the RBNZ in the October meeting though was to be careful about pricing in "done
deals" and that the outlook will be data-dependent. As we noted at the time, alongside measures of
inflation expectations, the key domestic data to watch ahead of the RBNZ’s 9 December meeting will
be indicators of what is happening to excess capacity (the output gap) as this will be a key determinant
of the Bank’s assessment of the outlook for inflation over the coming year.
It’s useful to remember in the RBNZ’s forecasting framework that GDP figures are the main
determinant of excess capacity in the economy (i.e., the output gap). The Q2 GDP data told us that the
output gap probably narrowed a bit through Q2, mainly reflecting historical revisions. In light of today’s
labour market numbers, the forecast for growth in Q3 may have some downside risk - but there are
conflicting indicators there, so it’s not that clear. However, it’s probably hard to argue that the level of
excess capacity reduced further in Q3 given today’s data. 
In the eyes of dovish MPC members, the rise in the unemployment rate and other measures important
for the RBNZ's labour market dashboard likely push in the direction of having greater comfort with the
medium-term inflation outlook. The vacancy to unemployment ratio increased a bit, labour constraints
eased a bit, and the unemployment rate has increased. Hence from a labour market perspective it
could be argued that policy could have more time all else equal. The outcome of the election, with the
possibility of a new RBNZ mandate, could increase the prominence of these factors - although this
won’t likely be an explicit factor in the RBNZ’s thinking in December even though it may be in the minds
of individual MPC members.
It could be the case that the argument for increases in the OCR track through 2027 could be
undermined with this data. It’s important to note (probably self-evidently) that there are other data that
will also be relevant. Hence the risk of a pause earlier in the New Year has probably increased with this
data, all else equal. I don't think our forecast peak OCR is really at issue here, but the time taken to get
there could be more flexible. Takes along the lines of "3% is the terminal rate" are very premature and
misplaced, in our view.

A December hike remains more likely than not, but there will be a clear debate to be had between the
MPC hawks and doves.
The forecast peak OCR is likely less impacted than the time to reach that peak. Further pauses in the
tightening cycle could come earlier than currently forecast. 
The outlook for the exchange rate is especially clouded by this data should it take a longer time for
NZ rates to move closer to global norms. 
Watch for updated RBNZ commentary on 10 and 19 November. The Governor’s observation that it
would have been useful to update markets ahead of the October meeting could imply a greater
willingness to comment in the coming weeks.

### Page 3

The outlook for the exchange rate is undermined by this data. I feel very happy about our year-end
target of 79c for the NZD/AUD rate with the risk that a recovery in the NZD takes longer than currently
assumed.
Looking ahead, there are some opportunities for the RBNZ to update markets on their views. Top of
mind is Paul Conway's 10 November speech to the UBS Australasia Conference. The Governor is also
speaking to the Institute of Directors on 19 November. If the RBNZ has something to say, then it might
happen in conjunction with these events (the 10 November speech is titled “How is the NZ economy
tracking”, so it will be difficult for Conway to avoid saying something about how he is interpreting
today’s data). We also note the Governor's comments in the October MPR that it could have been
useful to have been more flexible about updating markets on emerging views of new economic data.
This might increase the likelihood that these events could be used to provide guidance on the RBNZ’s
views of this data (and other emerging events), especially if it feels that the market’s interpretation is at
odds with its own.

Media contact
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist

## Document 409: Semiconductors Are Still Cyclical, But With A Bullish Long-Term Trend

Source PDF: all report pdf/Semiconductors Are Still Cyclical, But With A Bullish Long-Term Trend.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-10
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Semiconductors Are Still Cyclical, But With A Bullish Long-Term Trend
Date:    7 hours ago
Semiconductors Are Still Cyclical, But With A Bullish Long-Term Trend

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Semiconductors Are Still
Cyclical, But With A Bullish
Long-Term Trend
By Ed Yardeni & 2 others • 9 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by BoliviaInteligente / Unsplash

I. Semis Cycle
Historically, the semiconductor industry has been notoriously cyclical.
When chip demand outpaces supply, shortages boost prices; higher
prices in turn slow demand. High prices boost profit margins and stimulate
manufacturers to expand capacity. As demand moderates and new supply
comes online, pricing power weakens, and earnings growth slows or even

### Page 2

turns negative. In other words, the best cure for high chip prices is high
chip prices.
The AI boom has created an extraordinary imbalance between chip
demand and supply, fueling surging semiconductor prices, revenues,
earnings, and stock prices alike. But a key divergence has emerged
recently. Forward earnings growth for the companies in the S&P 500
Semiconductors industry has soared to 175% y/y, while the gains in the
industry's stock index plummeted from 130% y/y earlier this year to 9%

currently (chart). Historically, the y/y percent change in the Semiconductor
stock price index has tended to peak before earnings growth peaked and
bottom before it recovered. The latest divergence suggests that investors
may already be anticipating a peak in earnings growth.
We think investors may be partially right: Demand for chips should
continue rising as the AI revolution drives ever-greater demand for
computing power; that should propel earnings growth. However, financial,

political, and physical constraints are creating speed bumps for the AI
investment boom, which should moderate the pace of growth in both chip
demand and earnings.
One physical constraint is expanding semiconductor manufacturing
capacity, which should gradually increase supply. As the gap between
demand and supply narrows, pricing power and profit margins could come
under pressure. That would slow earnings growth.

### Page 3

That said, memory makers have sharply increased their use of long-term
agreements (LTAs) with hyperscalers, moving from quarterly or annual
contracts to three- to five-year deals backed by substantial prepayments,
which should help cushion a potential slowdown. Micron alone reported
roughly $150 billion in contracted future revenue last quarter.
The Semis' earnings boom has been fueled by both remarkable revenue
growth and expanding profit margins. Forward revenues is up 121.2% y/y,

while forward earnings has grown even faster at 175.1% (chart).
As a result, the industry's forward profit margin has reached a record
51.8%, nearly doubling from its early-2023 level (chart). These
extraordinary margins reflect the pricing power that semiconductor
companies have enjoyed as demand has outstripped available supply.

### Page 4

The anticipation of peak earnings growth may have contributed to the
recent drop in semiconductor stock prices. The S&P 500 Semiconductors
stock price index has plunged roughly 27% from its recent peak through
November 6, falling below its 200-day moving average (chart).
Nevertheless, its uptrend remains intact, in our opinion.
The selloff has also been evident in Semiconductor ETFs, which have
retreated sharply from their recent highs (chart).
II. Earnings & Valuation

Despite the recent selloff, semiconductor fundamentals remain strong.
Forward revenues per share for the S&P 500 Semiconductors industry
has soared to a new record high, reflecting the extraordinary demand for
chips generated by the AI investment boom (chart).

### Page 5

Forward earnings per share has climbed even faster, as the wide gap
between chip demand and supply has given manufacturers significant
pricing power and driven profit margins higher (chart).
Soaring earnings estimates and the recent decline in stock prices have
compressed valuations. The industry's forward P/E has plunged to just
13.1, well below the S&P 500's 18.7 and the Information Technology
sector's 18.4 (chart). Historically, Semiconductor P/Es have tended to

bottom near earnings-growth peaks and recover as earnings-growth
expectations bottom and turn higher. We suspect that such a recovery
could cushion stock prices as forward earnings growth moderates to more
sustainable levels, with speed bumps slowing—but not derailing—the AI
investment boom.

### Page 6

III. Fundamentals
The AI boom continues to fuel growth in global semiconductor sales,
which reached another record in September (chart). The three-month
average through September was $172.6 billion, or over $2.0 trillion
annually. Much of the recent surge is due to higher prices, as the industry
is capacity constrained.
Yet the US producer price index for semiconductors and related devices
fell 2.2% y/y in September (chart). The broad index is declining despite a

chip shortage as shortages are concentrated in advanced AI chips and
memory, while pricing conditions for conventional semiconductors remain
weaker.

### Page 7

Surging AI demand has created acute shortages for memory chips,
pushing their prices sharply higher. JP Morgan estimates that DRAM
prices will rise by more than 400% from the start of 2024 through the end
of 2026. These price pressures are also feeding into prices for computers
and related equipment (chart).
IV. Market Cap & Earnings Shares
Semiconductors have become the dominant earnings growth engine of
the Information Technology sector. Historically, their shares of the sector's

forward earnings and market capitalization moved largely in lockstep. That
relationship has recently broken down, as investors anticipate a peak in
earnings growth, leading to multiple compression. Semiconductors now
account for a record 54.8% of the sector's forward earnings, compared

### Page 8

with 41.0% of its market capitalization (chart). Prior to 2023, when the AI
boom started taking off, there was less of a gap between these two
shares, with both fluctuating between 10% and 20%.
The same pattern is evident across the S&P 500, where Semiconductors
accounts for 20.4% of forward earnings and just 15.9% of market
capitalization (chart). Nevertheless, these figures underscore the
industry's growing importance to the broader market, since both shares

fluctuated mostly below 4% prior to 2023.
The Semiconductors industry’s forward revenues is growing far faster than
those of the broader Information Technology sector and S&P 500 (chart).
Cyclical forces are likely to moderate the former.

### Page 9

The outperformance is even more striking in forward earnings growth
(chart). Again, cyclical forces are bound to slow this down.
The bottom line is that the Semiconductors industry remains cyclical, but
with a very bullish long-term trend. Accordingly, we view the latest selloff
as a buying opportunity.
💡

leave comments or questions at the end of our QuickTakes, and it
may be featured in the next "Just Ask Dr. Ed!"

### Page 10

clause.

《銀行獲利穩增長，利收動能接棒領航，資本市場
短暫休整》
大型銀行 3Q26獲利成長延續 ，但幅度收斂 ，主要
是非利息收入表現開始降溫，多數銀行認為投行案
源充足，需求只是遞延而非消失，債權相關業績因
利率走高而下滑，但股權相關業績仍有收獲；相比
之下，3Q獲利貢獻焦點一致轉向了淨利息收入 ，
業績增長得益於貸款與存款穩定成長，而財富管理
業務也提供了顯著的成長。部份銀行非利息費用成
長幅度大於收入成長幅度，導致效率比率變差。儘
管信貸品質仍健康無虞，但仍有部份銀行對潛在風
險展現謹慎態度，因而提高壞帳準備金。

展望後市，因淨利息收入的前景相對清晰，故可見
美大型銀行因此維持或上調 2026全年淨利息收入
預期，或上修 2026年股東權益報酬率 ，並重申或
下修壞帳率。相比之下，資本市場業務則需觀望，

## Document 502: グラフで見る景気予報 (2026年11月)

Source PDF: all report pdf/グラフで見る景気予報 (2026年11月).pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 23
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

3カ月前～ 現況 ～ 3カ月後 3カ月前～ 現況 ～3カ月後
景気全般 輸出
個人消費 輸入
住宅投資 生産
設備投資 雇用
公共投資 賃金
2026 年 11 月 4 日

調査レポート

グラフで見る景気予報 (11 月)

調査部 
【今月の景気判断】 
国内景気は、米国とイランの対立が逆風となっているものの、政府の物価高対策や代替調達の進展などが下
支えとなり、緩やかに持ち直している。内需においては、物価高を背景に家計の節約志向が根強いものの、雇
用・所得環境の改善が下支えとなり、個人消費は緩やかに持ち直している。他方、企業部門では、投資意欲は良
好なものの、設備や建設資材価格の上昇を受けて、設備投資は横ばいとなっている。外需については、日中関
係の悪化によりインバウンド需要の増勢が鈍化しているものの、トランプ関税の影響で落ち込んでいた米国向け
輸出が持ち直していることなどから、全体としては底堅く推移している。こうした動きを受けて、生産にも持ち直し
の動きがみられる。 
今後は、供給制約懸念の後退や良好な雇用・所得環境、堅調な企業業績を背景に、個人消費や設備投資と
いった内需が持ち直すことで、景気は緩やかな持ち直しを続けると見込まれる。世界的な AI 関連需要の堅調さ
に支えられて、輸出が底堅く推移することも下支えとなるだろう。 
ただし、景気の下振れリスクも依然として残っている。米国とイランの対立が長期化する中、原油・石油製品価
格の高止まりや円安進行によるインフレの再加速、原油やナフサといった輸入原材料不足による供給制約の再
燃には注意が必要である。また、日中関係の悪化に伴うインバウンド需要の低迷や戦略物資の輸出規制の強化、
米国の通商政策をめぐる不透明感、AI ブームへの期待剥落による混乱、金利の予期せぬ上昇による住宅投資
や設備投資の抑制、人手不足の深刻化、地震・天候不順なども、景気の下押し要因として意識される。

【今月の景気予報】

（注）1．3 カ月前～、～3 カ月後は｢現況｣の変化の方向感（改善、横ばい、悪化）を表し、            
「現況｣は現在の水準を天気マークで表す。 
2．シャドー部分は、前月から矢印および天気マークを変更した項目を表す。

### Page 2

 企業部門～企業の業績・設備投資の動向、人手不足・賃上げへの対応 
 海外経済～トランプ米大統領の政策と世界経済の先行き、米国の金融政策の行方、ウクライナ情勢の動向 
 その他～イラン情勢の行方、日中関係、高市政権による経済政策の動向、日本銀行による利上げや長期金
利の動向、円安の影響、AI ブームの行方、天候不順

### Page 5

1. 景気全般 ～緩やかに持ち直している 
2026 年 4～6 月期の実質 GDP 成長率（2 次速報）は前期比＋0.4%（年率換算＋1.4%）と 3 四半期連続のプ
ラス成長となった。イラン情勢の悪化によって物価高の加速や供給制約などに対する懸念が強まったが、政府の
対策が下支えとなり、外需を中心に底堅く推移した。今後も緩やかな回復基調が続くと期待されるが、米・イラン
の対立長期化に伴う原油価格の高止まりや供給制約懸念の再燃による下押しリスクには警戒が必要だろう。

### Page 6

9 月調査の日銀短観における大企業の業況判断 DI は、製造業では、イラン情勢の悪化を受けた供給制約懸
念が和らぐ中、コスト増加分の価格転嫁が進んだことから、石油・石炭製品などを中心に改善した。一方、非製造
業はコスト増加や地震・長雨などが下押し要因となり、宿泊・飲食サービスなどを中心に悪化した。先行きは、米
国とイランの対立長期化リスクが意識され、製造業、非製造業ともに悪化と、慎重な見通しとなった。

日銀短観（業況判断 DI）

（注１）シャドー部分は内閣府による景気後退期 
（注２）2004年、07年、10年、15年、18年、22年、24年、26年の各 3月調査時点で
調査対象企業の見直しが行われたため、前後の系列にギャップがある。 
（出所）日本銀行「全国企業短期経済観測調査」

4～6 月期の企業業績（金融業・保険業を除く）は、売上高が前期比＋2.7%と 4 四半期連続で増加し、利益率
も前期から改善したため、経常利益は同＋11.0%と 4 四半期連続で増加した（業種別の経常利益は、製造業で
は前期比＋12.1%、非製造業では同＋10.2%）。今後も米国とイランの対立長期化による資源価格の高止まりな
どが下押し要因となるものの、国内景気の持ち直しに支えられて底堅く推移するとみられる。

### Page 7

8 月の CI 一致指数は前月差－1.8 ポイントと 6 カ月ぶりに低下した。発表済み 9 系列全てがマイナスに寄与
した。中でも鉱工業用生産財出荷指数や生産指数（鉱工業）のマイナス寄与が大きかった。9 月の CI 一致指数
は生産指数（鉱工業）や鉱工業用生産財出荷指数のプラス寄与が大きく、 2 カ月ぶりに上昇すると見込まれる。
基調判断は「改善」が維持されよう。

景気動向指数（CI・DI）

（出所）内閣府「景気動向指数」

9 月の景気ウォッチャー調査の現状判断 DI（季節調整値）は 47.0 と 5 カ月連続で上昇した（前月差＋0.6 ポ
イント）。家計動向関連は、シルバーウィークの日並びの良さなど受けて、サービス関連を中心に改善した。企業
動向関連は、物価上昇に伴うコスト増加を受けて、製造業を中心に悪化した。先行き判断 DI（季節調整値）は 6
カ月ぶりに低下しており、コスト増加に伴い、採算の確保が難しくなるという見方がマインドを押し下げている。

景気ウォッチャー調査（季節調整値）

（注）景気の現状判断 DI は、家計動向関連業種、企業動向関連業種、雇用関連業種の景気ウォッチャ
ーによる景気判断から算出される。50 を上回ると「景気が良くなっている」、50 を下回ると「景気が
悪くなっている」という判断が優勢であることを示す。

### Page 8

2. 生産 ～持ち直しの動き 
9 月の鉱工業生産は前月比＋2.2%と 3 カ月ぶりに増加した。鉄鋼・非鉄金属工業などは減少した一方、自動
車工業や生産用機械工業などが増加し、全体を押し上げた。製造業の生産活動には持ち直しの動きがみられる。
製造工業生産予測調査では 10 月は前月比＋5.1%（同調査をもとにした経済産業省の予測は同＋3.9%）、11
月は同＋0.8%の計画だが、米・イランの対立長期化や相次ぐ自然災害の影響などで下振れる可能性がある。

### Page 9

9 月の鉱工業出荷は、自動車工業や汎用・業務用機械工業などを中心に前月比＋0.6%と 2 カ月ぶりに増加
した。また、前年比では生産用機械工業などを中心に＋0.2%と 4 カ月連続で増加した。在庫は鉄鋼・非鉄金属
工業や自動車工業などを中心に前月比＋2.5%と 2 カ月ぶりに増加した。また、前年比では自動車工業などを中
心に＋0.1%と 20 カ月ぶりに増加した。

在庫循環図（左）および同概念図（右）

（注）数値は四半期・原数値の前年比、在庫は期末値 
           （出所）経済産業省「鉱工業指数」

8 月の第 3 次産業活動指数は、前月比－0.1%と 2 カ月ぶりに低下した。広義対個人サービスは低下した一
方、広義対事業所サービスは上昇した。具体的には、運輸業・郵便業や情報通信業などは上昇したものの、金
融業・保険業や小売業などが低下し、全体を押し下げた。均してみれば、緩やかに上昇している。今後も国内景
気の持ち直しに併せて上昇傾向で推移すると期待されるが、物価高などを受けて下振れるリスクもある。

### Page 10

3. 雇用 ～改善している 
9 月の完全失業率は 2.4%と低水準で推移している。就業者（前月差＋34 万人）、雇用者（同＋35 万人）が増
加する一方、失業者（同－10 万人）は減少したことで、失業率の水準はコロナショック後の最低水準に並んだ。労
働需給のタイトな状態に変化はない。人件費の増加などから中小企業を中心に採用が慎重化している可能性が
あるが、それでも人手不足感は依然として強く、雇用情勢は良好な状態を維持しよう。

失業率と就業者数

（出所）総務省「労働力調査」

9 月の有効求人倍率は前月の 1.18 倍から 1.19 倍に上昇した。有効求人数（前月比＋0.0%）が横ばいだった
のに対し、有効求職者数（同－ 1.4%）が減少した。新規求人倍率は 2.19 倍に上昇した。新規求人数（前月比  
＋1.5%）が増加する一方で、新規求職申込件数（同－4.4% ）が落ち込んだ。人手不足は深刻化しているが、賃
金上昇を背景に企業は新規採用に慎重になっている可能性があり、有効求人倍率は緩やかに低下していこう。

有効求人倍率と新規求人倍率

### Page 11

4. 賃金 ～増加している 
8 月の一人当たり現金給与総額は前年比＋3.9%と堅調な増加が続いている。所定内給与（前年比＋3.8%）、
所定外給与（同＋4.9%）、特別給与（同＋1.8% ）のいずれも増加した。また、物価上昇率鈍化もあり、実質賃金
は同＋1.6%と 8 カ月連続でプラスとなった。良好な雇用情勢を背景に今後も賃金の増加基調は維持されるが、
目先は物価上昇ペースが高まることで実質賃金のプラス幅は縮小する可能性がある。

現金給与総額と所定内・所定外給与

（注）現金給与総額、所定内給与は名目賃金指数から、所定外給与は月間現金給与額から計算  
（出所）厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計」

5. 個人消費 ～緩やかに持ち直している 
8 月の二人以上世帯の実質消費支出は前月比＋0.1%と増加した。また日本の世帯全体の消費動向を表す 8
月の総消費動向指数（実質）は前月比＋0.1%と増加した。基調としては、個人消費は緩やかに持ち直している。
実質雇用者報酬は引き続き緩やかな増加を続けているのに加え、消費者態度指数からみた消費者マインドは持
ち直していることから、個人消費は当面、緩やかな持ち直しが続こう。

9 月の商業販売額（小売業）は前年比＋2.3%と 7 カ月連続で増加した。内訳をみると、自動車小売業、各種商
品小売業、飲食料品小売業、織物・衣服・身の回り品小売業などは増加した一方、機械器具小売業、燃料小売
業、医薬品・化粧品小売業は減少した。季節調整値は前月比－0.7%と 2 カ月連続で減少した。10 月の新車販
売台数（乗用車、除く軽自動車）は前年比＋1.1%と増加した。

個人消費関連指標

（出所）日本自動車販売協会連合会「自動車国内販売」、経済産業省「商業動態統計」

10 月の消費者態度指数（二人以上の世帯、季節調整値）は前月差－0.4ポイントと 2 カ月連続で低下した。内
訳をみると、「雇用環境」、「暮らし向き」、「耐久消費財の買い時判断」、「収入の増え方」のいずれの意識指標も
低下した。消費者態度指数からみた消費者マインドは、均してみれば持ち直しており、良好な雇用・所得環境の
下、今後も持ち直しが続くと期待される。

### Page 13

6. 住宅投資 ～弱含んでいる 
4～6 月期の住宅投資（実質 GDP ベース、2 次速報）は前期比－0.6%と 3 四半期ぶりに減少した。9 月の住
宅着工戸数は前年比－2.7%と 6 カ月ぶりに減少した。季節調整済年率換算値では 71.4 万戸（前月比－5.2%）
と 3 カ月連続で減少した。均してみれば弱含んでいる。今後は金利上昇やイラン情勢悪化を受けた建設資材価
格の上昇などが下押し要因となる一方、所得環境の改善が下支えとなり、横ばい圏で推移すると見込まれる。

住宅着工

（出所）国土交通省「建築着工統計」

7. 設備投資 ～横ばい 
4～6 月期の設備投資（実質 GDP ベース、2 次速報）は前期比－0.9%と 2 四半期連続で減少した。設備投資
に先行する機械受注（船舶・電力を除く民需）は、8 月は前月比＋5.2%と 2 カ月ぶりに増加した。うち製造業は前
月比＋1.6%、非製造業（船舶・電力を除く）は同＋2.9%といずれも増加した。このところ設備投資は横ばいとなっ
ているが、投資意欲は良好であることから、今後は持ち直しへ向かうと期待される。

8. 公共投資 ～横ばい圏で推移している 
4～6 月期の公共投資（実質 GDP ベース、2 次速報）は、前期比－0.5%と 2 四半期ぶりに減少したが、均して
みれば横ばい圏で推移している。公共投資の先行指標である公共工事請負額は、9 月は国、地方のいずれから
も減少し、前年比－8.8%と 2 カ月連続で減少した。工事コストの増加を受けた公共投資デフレーターの上昇によ
って実質値の伸びが抑制される状況が続いており、今後も公共投資は横ばい圏で推移する可能性がある。

公共投資と公共工事請負額

（出所）内閣府「四半期別 GDP 速報」、北海道建設業信用保証、東日本建設業保証、 
    西日本建設業保証「公共工事前払金保証統計」

9. 輸出入・国際収支 ～輸出、輸入ともに横ばい 
9 月の実質輸出は前月比－0.2%と 2 カ月連続で減少した。均してみれば、横ばいとなっている。好調な AI 関
連需要が下支えとなる中、イラン情勢悪化の影響が緩和へ向かうことで、持ち直しへ向かうと期待される。また、 9
月の実質輸入は前月比－0.8%と 2 カ月ぶりに減少した。均してみれば、横ばいとなっている。当面、中東からの
輸入が伸び悩む一方、代替輸入の増加が見込まれることから、横ばい圏で推移する可能性がある。

10. 物価 ～企業物価は急上昇、消費者物価は上昇 
9 月の国内企業物価は前年比＋6.8%と、電力・都市ガス・水道などを中心に前年比でプラスが続いている。一
方、9 月の消費者物価は、「生鮮食品を除く総合」が前年比＋2.1 %、「生鮮食品及びエネルギーを除く総合」が
同＋2.4%と、上昇が続いている。政府による種々の物価高対策により当面は安定した推移が見込まれるものの、
原油価格の上昇が企業のコスト転嫁を通じて物価の上振れにつながる可能性がある。

国内物価

（出所）日本銀行「企業物価指数」、総務省「消費者物価指数月報」

11. 世界景気 ～米国は堅調、欧州も底堅いが、中国は弱い 
世界景気は米国は堅調で欧州も底堅いが、中国は弱い。7～9 月期の実質 GDP 成長率は、米国は前期比年
率＋3.4%に加速した。個人消費が好調なほか、設備投資も同＋12.7%と 6 年ぶりの高い伸びとなった。欧州も同
＋1.7%と、高成長となった前期から鈍化したが底堅い。中国は前年比＋4.5%と、低い伸びにとどまった前期から
小幅加速したが依然として弱い。先行き、米国は堅調で欧州も底堅いが、中国は弱い動きが続く見込みである。

米国・欧州・中国の実質 GDP 成長率

### Page 18

12. 世界の物価 ～原油価格上昇の影響が懸念材料 
世界の消費者物価上昇率は、米国、欧州ではエネルギー価格などを中心に高水準で推移している。9 月は米
国で前年比＋3.7%と 4 カ月ぶりに加速し、ユーロ圏も同＋3.8% と 3 年ぶりの高い上昇率となった。中国も同   
＋0.9%と小幅加速した。先行き、原油価格上昇の影響により、世界の消費者物価上昇率は一段と上昇する見込
みである。 
米国・欧州・中国の消費者物価上昇率

（出所）米労働省、欧州連合統計局、中国国家統計局 
13. 原油 ～上昇 
10 月のブレント原油は上昇した。トランプ米大統領による米中間選挙後のイラン攻撃再開検討との報道、中国
による石油製品輸出の制限、ウクライナによるロシア製油所への攻撃、フーシ派とサウジアラビアとの攻撃の応酬
の激化などが相場押し上げ材料になった。足元で米国とイランの交渉再開に向けた動きやロシアとウクライナの
一部停戦といった対立緩和の材料もあるものの、総じて不透明感が強く、原油相場は不安定な推移となろう。

原油価格の推移

### Page 19

14. 国内金利～低下 
10 月の長期金利（新発 10 年国債利回り）は低下。植田日銀総裁が講演で基調的な物価上昇率が目標の2%
に近づいていると述べたことで利上げ強化観測が強まり一時3.115%まで上昇したが、AI 関連企業の資金繰り懸
念により日米のハイテク関連株が急落し、投資家のリスク回避姿勢が強まったことで低下に転じ、下旬には一時
2.755%まで低下した。ただし、財政悪化、物価上昇への警戒感などから金利の低下余地は限られよう。

国内金利

（出所）日本銀行

15. 米国金利 ～低下 
10 月の米長期金利は低下。インフレ懸念により FRB（米連邦準備制度理事会）の金融引き締め姿勢が強まる
との観測から一時 5.36%まで上昇したが、AI 関連企業の資金繰り懸念からハイテク関連株が急落したことで安全
資産として債券買いが広がったこと、10 月 FOMC（米連邦公開市場委員会）で利上げが見送られるとの観測が
強まったことで下旬に 4.89%台まで低下した。もっともインフレ圧力は強く、金利が上昇しやすい地合いが続こう。 
米国金利

### Page 20

16. 国内株価～下落 
10 月の日経平均株価は、AI 関連銘柄の値動きに振り回される展開が続く中、大幅下落。上旬は米株高を受
け AI 関連株中心に買われ一時 7 万円の大台を回復したが、フランスの財政懸念を背景とした世界的な金利上
昇、AI インフラへの巨額投資に対する懸念による AI 関連株の下落、中東情勢の緊迫化による原油高への懸念
から、下旬には一時 6 万 1 千円台まで下落した。AI 関連株を中心に当面荒っぽい値動きが続こう。

日本株価

（出所）日本経済新聞

17. 米国株価～上昇後、下落 
10 月の米国株価は上昇後、下落。上旬は、利上げ観測の後退や大手企業の決算への期待からAI 関連株中
心に買われたが、クラウドサービス会社のコアウィーブの信用不安をきっかけとして巨額の AI 関連投資の持続性
への懸念が高まり、月末にかけて AI 関連株を中心に反落した。中東情勢の悪化への警戒感も売り材料となった。
巨額の AI 関連投資への不安、金利上昇、物価高懸念を受けて、当面荒っぽい値動きが続こう。

米国株価

### Page 21

18. 為替～横ばい 
10 月の円は対米ドルで 157～158 円台を中心に横ばいで推移した。上旬は日銀の追加利上げ観測が高まっ
た一方、9 月米雇用統計の弱めの結果を受けて FRB の追加利上げ観測は後退したため、一時 157 円を割り込
む水準まで円高が進んだが、 その後はイエメンの親イラン武装組織フーシ派がサウジアラビアの首都リヤドの国
際空港を攻撃するなど中東情勢の悪化懸念から原油価格が上昇したこと、9 月米 CPI の高めの結果を受けて米
利上げ観測が再浮上したことから、一時 159 円近辺まで弱含んだ。しかし、AI 関連銘柄を中心に米株価が下落
したことを背景に米長期金利が節目の 5%を下回ったことから反発し、一時 156 円近辺と約 1 カ月ぶりの円高水
準を付けた。もっとも積極的な円買い材料も乏しく、日米とも政策金利が据え置かれる中で、月末までレンジ内で
のもみ合いが続いた。米中間選挙の結果や中東情勢の行方をにらみつつ、しばらくはレンジ内での推移が続こう。

### Page 22

19. 金融～マネーストックは緩やかに増加し、銀行貸出残高は堅調に増加 
日銀は 2026 年 9 月の金融政策決定会合において、基調的な物価上昇率が 2%の物価安定目標を超えて上
振れしていくリスクがあるとして、無担保コールレート（オーバーナイト物）の目標水準を 1.25%に引き上げた（今
次利上げ局面では 2024 年 7 月、2025 年 1 月、12 月、2026 年 6 月に続き 5 回目）。また、10 月の決定会合で
は政策金利は据え置いたが、基調的な消費者物価の上昇率は 2%の物価安定の目標と概ね整合的な水準とな
っているとし、2013 年 1 月の物価安定目標の導入から 13 年間かけて、ようやく目標に到達したと表明した。今後
は、現在の金融環境は緩和的であるとして、基調的な物価上昇率を 2%程度の水準で安定させていくために、引
き続き政策金利を引き上げる方針を示している。金融を巡る動きとしては、 9 月マネーストック（M2 ）は前年比  
＋2.0%と緩やかに増加している一方、9 月銀行貸出残高は同＋5.7%と堅調に増加している。

景気予報ガイド 
【今月の景気判断】 
○レポート発表時における景気判断。【今月の景気予報】における各項目の現況をもとに総合的に判断。

【今月の景気予報】 
○3 カ月前～・・・3 カ月前～現況の「方向感」を、①改善、②横ばい、③悪化の 3 段階に分類。 
○現況 ・・・・・各項目の現在の「水準」を、①晴れ、②曇り、③雨の 3 段階に分類。 
○～3 カ月後・・・現況～3 カ月後の「方向感」を、①改善、②横ばい、③悪化の 3 段階に分類。

（注）現況をあらわす天気マークはあくまで「水準」を判断したもので、3 カ月前～の矢印が①改善、③悪化の場合で
も、必ずしも天気マークが変更されるわけではない。

【当面の注目材料】 
○各項目の現況、見通しをふまえて、今後景気を判断する上で注目すべきポイント。

【各項目のヘッドライン】 
○全 19 項目について、毎月簡単なヘッドラインを記載、当月と前月のものを対比して示している。 
○右端は各項目のグラフが掲載されているページ数。

【前月からの変更点】 
○景気予報において判断の変更があった場合に変更理由を記載。 
○ヘッドラインに記載されている市場データ（原油・金利・株価・為替）については記載せず。

【主要経済指標の推移】 
○当レポートで用いられている経済指標の半年間の動きを掲載。

## Document 536: 两大巨头控盘，伦敦铝市场遭遇数十年来最严峻“逼空”

Source PDF: all report pdf/两大巨头控盘，伦敦铝市场遭遇数十年来最严峻“逼空”.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-11
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

伦敦铝库存已跌⾄ 1990 年以来最低⽔平，两名交易商持有的多头头⼨合计占据交易所现成
库存的 60% ⾄ 80% ，市场流动性急剧收紧。伦敦现货铝合约价格周三⼀度较三个⽉期货基
准溢价⾼达每吨 147 美元，刷新逾 30 年数据记录。
摘要：
伦敦⾦属交易所铝市场正⾯临数⼗年来最严峻的供应挤压，现货价格⼀度创历史纪录⾼溢价，
市场紧绷程度引发⼴泛关注。
现货铝合约价格周三⼀度较三个⽉期货基准溢价⾼达每吨 147 美元，刷新逾 30 年数据记录。
与此同时， LME 铝库存已跌⾄ 1990 年以来最低⽔平，两名交易商持有的多头头⼨合计占据交易
所现成库存的 60% ⾄ 80% ，市场流动性急剧收紧。
这场供应挤压的影响已蔓延⾄整个铝产业链。中东地区战事扰乱了供货路线，美欧贸易壁垒令
替代供应来源有限。印度尼西亚产能虽已开始加速释放，但其⾦属尚未完成 LME 交割注册，短
期内仍难以缓解现货市场压⼒。
LME ⽅⾯表⽰，交易所已建⽴⼀系列机制以防范⼤规模或主导性头⼨产⽣不当影响，并有权要
求市场参与者对相关头⼨进⾏管理。

尽管周三 " 次⽇ / 隔夜 " （ Tom/next ）价差从最⾼每吨 31.73 美元收窄⾄ 1 美元，短期压⼒有所缓
解，但期货曲线显⽰，升⽔结构（ backwardation ）将延续⾄ 2028 年。
现货溢价创历史纪录，库存跌⾄三⼗年低点
周三， LME 铝现货合约对三个⽉期货的溢价攀升⾄每吨 147 美元，为逾 30 年来最⾼⽔平。就在今
年 10 ⽉中旬，这⼀价差还处于贴⽔状态，此后随着交易所仓库库存骤降⽽急速翻转。
衡量短线资⾦成本的 Tom/next 价差亦⼀度触及每吨 31.73 美元，为 2021 年以来最⾼，直观反映出
交易商争夺剩余⾦属交割权的激烈程度。
LME 铝市场这种现货升⽔结构是 1980 年代末以来最极端的⼀次。彼时， Marc Rich + Co 主导了
⼀场逼仓⾏动，将现货与三个⽉期货价差推升⾄每吨逾 1000 美元。
此后 LME 引⼊新规，强制要求持有⼤规模头⼨的交易商向市场借出仓单，以此为 backwardation
的幅度设置上限。
两⼤交易商控制逾六成可⽤库存

供应紧张的核⼼在于库存的⾼度集中。 LME 交易所数据显⽰，截⾄本周⼀，两个实体持有的仓
单及现货合约多头头⼨，合计相当于交易所全球仓储⽹络中现成可⽤库存的 60% ⾄ 80% 。
对于需要现货⾦属的买家⽽⾔，这意味着市场上可供出售的卖家寥寥⽆⼏。持有空头头⼨的机
构所⾯临的压⼒尤为突出 —— 其合约到期时，要么必须回购头⼨平仓，要么须以现货⾦属完成
交割。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

写评论
图⽚
LME 在回应提问时表⽰，交易所已部署多项安排以防范⼤规模或主导性头⼨的不当影响，并会
常规性地向市场参与者索取更多头⼨管理信息，同时具备要求头⼨进⾏适当管理的权⼒。
若主导性多头头⼨在剩余库存中的占⽐进⼀步上升，交易所强制其以限价向市场借出头⼨的规
则可能随时触发。
供应替代渠道受阻，印尼增量产能短期难解近渴
伦敦市场的这场紧缺，折射出更⼴泛的全球铝供应链困境。
中东战事持续扰动了该地区的铝出⼝。尽管波斯湾供应商此后找到了替代物流路线，但这些路
线成本更⾼、⻛险更⼤、运距更⻓。部分冶炼⼚因遭受袭击尚在修复之中，出货能⼒受限。
美国和欧洲筑起的贸易壁垒则进⼀步压缩了全球现货市场的可⽤替代来源。
放眼中期，印度尼西亚的铝产能快速扩张被视为扭转全球铝市格局的关键变量。印尼出⼝量已

开始加速增⻓，市场预期这将在未来数年推动全球铝市由紧转供应过剩，这⼀前景也令近⼏个
⽉铝价承压。
然⽽，对于眼下急需现货⾦属的买家来说，困境仍难⾔短期化解：印尼铝⽬前尚未完成 LME 交
割注册，意味着这批新增供应暂时⽆法直接进⼊交易所体系，填补当前的库存缺⼝。
近期的核⼼问题在于， backwardation 结构需要攀升⾄何种程度，才能促使库存和现货合约的持
有者将头⼨向远期展期。

景気は弱い動き
中国景気は弱い動きが続いているが、改善の兆しもみられる。2026年7～9月期の実質GDP成長率は前年比＋4.5%
だった。前期（前年比＋4.3%）から伸びが拡大し、1～9月累計で同＋4.6%と政府目標の「4.5～5%」の範囲内で推移した。
ただし、10月の製造業PMIは49.3と前月から低下し、好不況の境目である50を2カ月ぶりに下回った。構成指数をみると、
生産と新規受注の低下が全体を押し下げた。非製造業PMIも49.7と低下し、50を下回った。

2026年7～9月期のGDP 外需の強さを背景に製造業が成長率を押し上げ

期連続で前年割れとなった。

国慶節の人出は増加も、財布のひもは依然固い
中国の建国記念日である国慶節（10/1）に伴う大型連休（10/1～7）の1日当たり国内旅行者数は、前年比＋6.3%と増加
した（左図）。休日日数は前年より1日短かったものの、今年は中秋節の連休が9月末にあり、間で休暇を取れば最大13連
休となる日並びだった。このため、海外旅行需要も高まり、連休中の1日当たり出入国者数は、前年比＋8.1%と増加した。
もっとも、連休中の1人1日当たり旅行消費額は2024年以降伸び悩んでおり、今年は前年比－1.9%と2年連続で減少した
（右図）。旅行需要は底堅いものの、消費者の節約志向は依然として根強い。
